3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: PHT100 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Engineering Physics A

Core physical principles for engineers: Simple harmonic motion, damped & forced oscillations, resonance, wave optics (interference, diffraction, polarization), lasers & holography, optical fiber communication, quantum mechanics (wave packets, Schrödinger wave equations), and nanoscience fundamentals.

Module 1

Oscillations, Resonance and Wave Motion

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

Question 1 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: PHY-Q1

Q1. Derive the differential equation of damped harmonic oscillations. Obtain the general solution and discuss the three cases of damping: overdamped, critically damped, and underdamped. Q1. Derive the differential equation of damped harmonic oscillations. Obtain the general solution and discuss the three cases of damping: overdamped, critically damped, and underdamped. Q1. Derive the differential equation of damped harmonic oscillations. Obtain the general solution and discuss the three cases of damping: overdamped, critically damped, and underdamped.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: January 2021 Improvement Exam (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 11(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + 2\gamma \frac{dx}{dt} + \omega_0^2 x = 0 \implies x(t) = A e^{-\gamma t}\cos(\omega t - \phi), \quad \omega = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \gamma^2}$$

Damped Motion Equation: $\ddot{x} + 2\gamma\dot{x} + \omega_0^2 x = 0$, where $\gamma = b/2m$ and $\omega_0 = \sqrt{k/m}$.
Three Regimes:
1. Overdamped ($\gamma > \omega_0$): Deadbeat exponential return without oscillation.
2. Critically Damped ($\gamma = \omega_0$): Fastest return to equilibrium without overshoot ($x = (C_1 + C_2 t)e^{-\gamma t}$).
3. Underdamped ($\gamma < \omega_0$): Oscillatory motion with decaying amplitude $A(t) = A_0 e^{-\gamma t}$ and frequency $\omega = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \gamma^2}$.
Relaxation Time: $\tau = 1/\gamma = 2m/b$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

When an oscillating mechanical system moves through a viscous medium, resistive dissipative forces oppose its motion. These damping forces arise from internal molecular friction or medium viscosity, progressively depleting mechanical energy and converting it into thermal energy. Consider a particle of mass $m$ oscillating along the $x$-axis. It experiences two concurrent forces: a linear elastic restoring force and a velocity-dependent viscous damping force.

According to Hooke's law, the restoring force is directed towards the mean equilibrium position: $$F_r = -k x$$ where $k$ represents the spring constant or force stiffness, and $x$ denotes the instantaneous displacement. Assuming viscous damping at moderate velocities, the dissipative damping force is directly proportional to the instantaneous velocity: $$F_d = -b \frac{dx}{dt} = -b \dot{x}$$ where $b$ is the damping coefficient. Applying Newton's second law of motion to the oscillating particle yields: $$m \frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} = F_r + F_d = -k x - b \frac{dx}{dt}$$ Rearranging all terms to the left-hand side gives: $$m \frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + b \frac{dx}{dt} + k x = 0$$ Dividing throughout by mass $m$: $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + \frac{b}{m} \frac{dx}{dt} + \frac{k}{m} x = 0$$ To express this differential equation in standard form, we define the damping constant $\gamma = \frac{b}{2m}$ and the undamped natural angular frequency $\omega_0 = \sqrt{\frac{k}{m}}$. The equation becomes: $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + 2\gamma \frac{dx}{dt} + \omega_0^2 x = 0$$

This is a homogeneous linear second-order differential equation with constant coefficients. Assuming a trial solution of the exponential form $x(t) = C e^{r t}$, its first and second time derivatives are $\frac{dx}{dt} = C r e^{rt}$ and $\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} = C r^2 e^{rt}$. Substituting these into the differential equation yields the characteristic algebraic equation: $$r^2 + 2\gamma r + \omega_0^2 = 0$$ Solving for the characteristic roots using the quadratic formula: $$r = -\gamma \pm \sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2}$$ The general solution is therefore the linear combination of the two independent solutions: $$x(t) = C_1 e^{\left(-\gamma + \sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2}\right)t} + C_2 e^{\left(-\gamma - \sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2}\right)t} = e^{-\gamma t} \left[ C_1 e^{\sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2}\,t} + C_2 e^{-\sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2}\,t} \right]$$ The physical nature of the resulting motion depends entirely on the sign of the discriminant $(\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2)$, giving rise to three distinct physical regimes:

Case 1: Overdamped Motion (Heavy Damping, $\gamma > \omega_0$). The quantity $(\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2)$ is positive, so the square root $\beta = \sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2}$ is a real number less than $\gamma$. Both roots $r_1 = -\gamma + \beta$ and $r_2 = -\gamma - \beta$ are purely real and negative. The displacement equation is: $$x(t) = e^{-\gamma t} \left( C_1 e^{\beta t} + C_2 e^{-\beta t} \right)$$ The displacement decays exponentially towards zero without ever crossing the equilibrium position more than once. The motion is completely non-oscillatory (deadbeat). A practical example is the motion of a door damper filled with heavy oil or a deadbeat moving-coil galvanometer.

Case 2: Critically Damped Motion ($\gamma = \omega_0$). The discriminant vanishes ($\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2 = 0$), yielding two identical repeated real roots $r_1 = r_2 = -\gamma$. The general solution for repeated roots takes the form: $$x(t) = (C_1 + C_2 t) e^{-\gamma t}$$ In this critical threshold regime, the displaced system returns to its equilibrium position in the absolute minimum possible time without overshooting. Critical damping is engineered into vehicle shock absorbers and sensitive measuring instruments to guarantee rapid, vibration-free settling.

Case 3: Underdamped Motion (Light Damping, $\gamma < \omega_0$). The discriminant $(\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2)$ is negative, making $\sqrt{\gamma^2 - \omega_0^2} = i \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \gamma^2} = i \omega$, where $\omega = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \gamma^2}$ represents the modified angular frequency of damped oscillations. Using Euler's identity $e^{\pm i \omega t} = \cos(\omega t) \pm i \sin(\omega t)$, the general solution simplifies to: $$x(t) = A e^{-\gamma t} \cos(\omega t - \phi)$$ where $A$ is the initial amplitude and $\phi$ is the initial phase angle, both determined by initial displacement and velocity conditions. The system executes true oscillatory motion, but the envelope of maximum amplitude decays exponentially according to $A(t) = A_0 e^{-\gamma t}$. The time required for the amplitude to drop to $1/e$ of its initial value is the relaxation time $\tau = \frac{1}{\gamma} = \frac{2m}{b}$.

Question 2 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: PHY-Q2

Q2. Derive the differential equation for forced harmonic oscillations. Obtain the expressions for the steady-state amplitude and phase difference, and discuss their variation with driving frequency. Q2. Derive the differential equation for forced harmonic oscillations. Obtain the expressions for the steady-state amplitude and phase difference, and discuss their variation with driving frequency. Q2. Derive the differential equation for forced harmonic oscillations. Obtain the expressions for the steady-state amplitude and phase difference, and discuss their variation with driving frequency.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: December 2021 Evaluation Scheme (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 11(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$A = \frac{f_0}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2}}, \quad \tan\phi = \frac{2\gamma \omega}{\omega_0^2 - \omega^2}$$

Steady-State Solution: $x(t) = A \sin(\omega t - \phi)$.
Phase Angle Across Frequencies:
1. $\omega \ll \omega_0 \implies \phi \to 0$ (displacement in phase with driving force, stiffness-controlled).
2. $\omega = \omega_0 \implies \phi = \pi/2$ (displacement lags force by $90^\circ$, force in phase with velocity, velocity resonance).
3. $\omega \gg \omega_0 \implies \phi \to \pi$ (displacement lags force by $180^\circ$, inertia-controlled).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In real physical systems, damped oscillations eventually die out due to energy dissipation. To sustain oscillations indefinitely, an external periodic driving force must continuously inject energy into the system. A forced harmonic oscillator is governed by three forces: a linear restoring force $-kx$, a viscous damping force $-b \dot{x}$, and an external harmonic driving force $F(t) = F_0 \sin(\omega t)$, where $F_0$ is the driving force amplitude and $\omega$ is the driving angular frequency.

Applying Newton's second law of motion to the forced oscillating particle yields: $$m \frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} = -k x - b \frac{dx}{dt} + F_0 \sin(\omega t)$$ Transposing all displacement-dependent terms to the left-hand side: $$m \frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + b \frac{dx}{dt} + k x = F_0 \sin(\omega t)$$ Dividing throughout by the mass $m$: $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + \frac{b}{m} \frac{dx}{dt} + \frac{k}{m} x = \frac{F_0}{m} \sin(\omega t)$$ Substituting the standard parameters $\gamma = \frac{b}{2m}$, $\omega_0^2 = \frac{k}{m}$, and driving acceleration amplitude $f_0 = \frac{F_0}{m}$, we obtain the canonical non-homogeneous differential equation of forced harmonic motion: $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + 2\gamma \frac{dx}{dt} + \omega_0^2 x = f_0 \sin(\omega t)$$

The complete mathematical solution consists of two components: the complementary function $x_c(t)$ (the transient response) and the particular integral $x_p(t)$ (the steady-state response). Because the transient response contains the exponential decay factor $e^{-\gamma t}$, it rapidly diminishes to zero over time. In steady-state operation, the system oscillates exclusively at the driving frequency $\omega$ with a phase lag $\phi$ behind the driving force. We propose a steady-state trial solution: $$x(t) = A \sin(\omega t - \phi)$$ Differentiating this trial displacement with respect to time gives: $$\frac{dx}{dt} = A \omega \cos(\omega t - \phi)$$ $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} = -A \omega^2 \sin(\omega t - \phi)$$ Substituting these expressions into the differential equation: $$-A \omega^2 \sin(\omega t - \phi) + 2\gamma A \omega \cos(\omega t - \phi) + \omega_0^2 A \sin(\omega t - \phi) = f_0 \sin(\omega t)$$ Regrouping terms: $$A (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) \sin(\omega t - \phi) + 2\gamma \omega A \cos(\omega t - \phi) = f_0 \sin[(\omega t - \phi) + \phi]$$ Expanding the right-hand side using the trigonometric addition formula $\sin(A + B) = \sin A \cos B + \cos A \sin B$: $$A (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) \sin(\omega t - \phi) + 2\gamma \omega A \cos(\omega t - \phi) = f_0 \cos\phi \sin(\omega t - \phi) + f_0 \sin\phi \cos(\omega t - \phi)$$ Equating coefficients of the orthogonal functions $\sin(\omega t - \phi)$ and $\cos(\omega t - \phi)$ on both sides: $$A (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) = f_0 \cos\phi \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ $$2\gamma \omega A = f_0 \sin\phi \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

To determine the steady-state amplitude $A$, we square both equations and add them together: $$A^2 (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2 A^2 = f_0^2 (\cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi) = f_0^2$$ Factoring out $A^2$: $$A^2 \left[ (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2 \right] = f_0^2$$ Taking the square root gives the steady-state amplitude expression: $$A = \frac{f_0}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2}} = \frac{F_0}{\sqrt{m^2(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + b^2 \omega^2}}$$ To obtain the phase lag $\phi$, we divide Equation 2 by Equation 1: $$\tan\phi = \frac{f_0 \sin\phi}{f_0 \cos\phi} = \frac{2\gamma \omega}{\omega_0^2 - \omega^2} \implies \phi = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{2\gamma \omega}{\omega_0^2 - \omega^2}\right)$$

Physical interpretation of phase angle across excitation frequencies: 1. Low driving frequencies ($\omega \ll \omega_0$): The denominator $(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) \approx \omega_0^2 > 0$ and the numerator is nearly zero. Thus $\tan\phi \to 0 \implies \phi \approx 0$. The displacement is almost perfectly in phase with the driving force, and the motion is stiffness-controlled. 2. Natural frequency excitation ($\omega = \omega_0$): The denominator vanishes while the numerator is positive. Thus $\tan\phi \to \infty \implies \phi = \frac{\pi}{2}$. The displacement lags the driving force by exactly $90^\circ$, meaning the driving force is perfectly aligned in phase with oscillator velocity $\dot{x}(t)$, ensuring maximum rate of mechanical energy transfer. 3. High driving frequencies ($\omega \gg \omega_0$): The denominator becomes large and negative. Thus $\tan\phi \to -0 \implies \phi \to \pi$. The displacement oscillates completely out of phase ($180^\circ$ lag) with the driving force, and motion is inertia-controlled.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

In real physical systems, damped oscillations eventually die out due to energy dissipation. To sustain oscillations indefinitely, an external periodic driving force must continuously inject energy into the system. A forced harmonic oscillator is governed by three forces: a linear restoring force $-kx$, a viscous damping force $-b \dot{x}$, and an external harmonic driving force $F(t) = F_0 \sin(\omega t)$, where $F_0$ is the driving force amplitude and $\omega$ is the driving angular frequency.

Applying Newton's second law of motion to the forced oscillating particle yields: $$m \frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} = -k x - b \frac{dx}{dt} + F_0 \sin(\omega t)$$ Transposing all displacement-dependent terms to the left-hand side: $$m \frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + b \frac{dx}{dt} + k x = F_0 \sin(\omega t)$$ Dividing throughout by the mass $m$: $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + \frac{b}{m} \frac{dx}{dt} + \frac{k}{m} x = \frac{F_0}{m} \sin(\omega t)$$ Substituting the standard parameters $\gamma = \frac{b}{2m}$, $\omega_0^2 = \frac{k}{m}$, and driving acceleration amplitude $f_0 = \frac{F_0}{m}$, we obtain the canonical non-homogeneous differential equation of forced harmonic motion: $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + 2\gamma \frac{dx}{dt} + \omega_0^2 x = f_0 \sin(\omega t)$$

The complete mathematical solution consists of two components: the complementary function $x_c(t)$ (the transient response) and the particular integral $x_p(t)$ (the steady-state response). Because the transient response contains the exponential decay factor $e^{-\gamma t}$, it rapidly diminishes to zero over time. In steady-state operation, the system oscillates exclusively at the driving frequency $\omega$ with a phase lag $\phi$ behind the driving force. We propose a steady-state trial solution: $$x(t) = A \sin(\omega t - \phi)$$ Differentiating this trial displacement with respect to time gives: $$\frac{dx}{dt} = A \omega \cos(\omega t - \phi)$$ $$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} = -A \omega^2 \sin(\omega t - \phi)$$ Substituting these expressions into the differential equation: $$-A \omega^2 \sin(\omega t - \phi) + 2\gamma A \omega \cos(\omega t - \phi) + \omega_0^2 A \sin(\omega t - \phi) = f_0 \sin(\omega t)$$ Regrouping terms: $$A (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) \sin(\omega t - \phi) + 2\gamma \omega A \cos(\omega t - \phi) = f_0 \sin[(\omega t - \phi) + \phi]$$ Expanding the right-hand side using the trigonometric addition formula $\sin(A + B) = \sin A \cos B + \cos A \sin B$: $$A (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) \sin(\omega t - \phi) + 2\gamma \omega A \cos(\omega t - \phi) = f_0 \cos\phi \sin(\omega t - \phi) + f_0 \sin\phi \cos(\omega t - \phi)$$ Equating coefficients of the orthogonal functions $\sin(\omega t - \phi)$ and $\cos(\omega t - \phi)$ on both sides: $$A (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) = f_0 \cos\phi \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ $$2\gamma \omega A = f_0 \sin\phi \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

To determine the steady-state amplitude $A$, we square both equations and add them together: $$A^2 (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2 A^2 = f_0^2 (\cos^2\phi + \sin^2\phi) = f_0^2$$ Factoring out $A^2$: $$A^2 \left[ (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2 \right] = f_0^2$$ Taking the square root gives the steady-state amplitude expression: $$A = \frac{f_0}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2}} = \frac{F_0}{\sqrt{m^2(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + b^2 \omega^2}}$$ To obtain the phase lag $\phi$, we divide Equation 2 by Equation 1: $$\tan\phi = \frac{f_0 \sin\phi}{f_0 \cos\phi} = \frac{2\gamma \omega}{\omega_0^2 - \omega^2} \implies \phi = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{2\gamma \omega}{\omega_0^2 - \omega^2}\right)$$

Physical interpretation of phase angle across excitation frequencies: 1. Low driving frequencies ($\omega \ll \omega_0$): The denominator $(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) \approx \omega_0^2 > 0$ and the numerator is nearly zero. Thus $\tan\phi \to 0 \implies \phi \approx 0$. The displacement is almost perfectly in phase with the driving force, and the motion is stiffness-controlled. 2. Natural frequency excitation ($\omega = \omega_0$): The denominator vanishes while the numerator is positive. Thus $\tan\phi \to \infty \implies \phi = \frac{\pi}{2}$. The displacement lags the driving force by exactly $90^\circ$, meaning the driving force is perfectly aligned in phase with oscillator velocity $\dot{x}(t)$, ensuring maximum rate of mechanical energy transfer. 3. High driving frequencies ($\omega \gg \omega_0$): The denominator becomes large and negative. Thus $\tan\phi \to -0 \implies \phi \to \pi$. The displacement oscillates completely out of phase ($180^\circ$ lag) with the driving force, and motion is inertia-controlled.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\omega_r = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2}, \quad Q = \frac{\omega_0}{2\gamma} = \frac{\omega_0}{\Delta \omega}, \quad \delta = \gamma T = \frac{\pi}{Q}$$

Resonant Frequency: Amplitude resonance occurs at $\omega_r = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2} < \omega_0$.
Quality Factor ($Q$): $Q = 2\pi \frac{\text{Energy Stored}}{\text{Energy Lost / Cycle}} = \frac{\omega_0}{\text{Bandwidth }\Delta\omega}$. Higher $Q$ means sharper resonance and lower damping.
Logarithmic Decrement ($\delta$): $\delta = \ln(A_n / A_{n+1}) = \gamma T = \pi / Q$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Resonance is the physical phenomenon where an oscillating system driven by an external periodic force responds with maximum amplitude when the excitation frequency approaches the system's natural frequency. Depending on the physical parameter observed, we distinguish between amplitude resonance (maximum displacement) and velocity resonance (maximum velocity).

To find the condition for amplitude resonance, we determine the driving frequency $\omega$ that maximizes the steady-state amplitude: $$A(\omega) = \frac{f_0}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2}}$$ Maximizing $A(\omega)$ is equivalent to minimizing the radicand function $f(\omega) = (\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)^2 + 4\gamma^2 \omega^2$ with respect to $\omega$. Differentiating and equating to zero: $$\frac{df}{d\omega} = 2(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2)(-2\omega) + 8\gamma^2 \omega = 0$$ Since $\omega \neq 0$, dividing by $4\omega$ gives: $$-(\omega_0^2 - \omega^2) + 2\gamma^2 = 0 \implies \omega^2 = \omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2$$ Therefore, the amplitude resonant angular frequency is: $$\omega_r = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2}$$ Notice that amplitude resonance occurs at a frequency strictly lower than the undamped natural frequency $\omega_0$. For resonance to be physically possible, $\omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2 > 0 \implies \gamma < \frac{\omega_0}{\sqrt{2}}$. Substituting $\omega_r$ back into the amplitude formula gives the peak resonant amplitude: $$A_{max} = \frac{f_0}{\sqrt{\left[\omega_0^2 - (\omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2)\right]^2 + 4\gamma^2(\omega_0^2 - 2\gamma^2)}} = \frac{f_0}{\sqrt{4\gamma^4 + 4\gamma^2 \omega_0^2 - 8\gamma^4}} = \frac{f_0}{2\gamma \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \gamma^2}}$$ For negligible or light damping ($\gamma \ll \omega_0$), this peak value simplifies to $A_{max} \approx \frac{f_0}{2\gamma \omega_0}$.

Sharpness of resonance refers to the rate at which the response amplitude falls off on either side of the resonant peak as the excitation frequency deviates from $\omega_0$. If a small shift away from resonance causes a steep decline in amplitude, the resonance is described as sharp; if the response varies sluggishly over a wide frequency band, the resonance is flat. Damping directly governs sharpness: low damping yields a narrow, tall peak, while heavy damping flattens the curve.

The sharpness of resonance is quantitatively measured by the Quality Factor ($Q$-factor). By fundamental definition, the $Q$-factor is $2\pi$ times the ratio of the total energy stored in the oscillator to the energy dissipated per cycle: $$Q = 2\pi \frac{\text{Stored Energy}}{\text{Energy Dissipated Per Cycle}} = \frac{\omega_0}{2\gamma} = \frac{m \omega_0}{b}$$ In the frequency domain, the $Q$-factor is expressed in terms of the resonant frequency $\omega_0$ and the half-power bandwidth $\Delta \omega = \omega_2 - \omega_1$: $$Q = \frac{\omega_0}{\Delta \omega} = \frac{f_0}{f_2 - f_1}$$ where $\omega_1$ and $\omega_2$ are the lower and upper half-power cut-off frequencies at which the power absorbed drops to half its maximum value, or equivalently where displacement amplitude falls to $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} A_{max}$. A higher $Q$-factor indicates superior frequency selectivity and low energy loss per oscillation cycle.

In decaying underdamped oscillations, the rate of amplitude attenuation is quantified by the Logarithmic Decrement ($\delta$). It is defined as the natural logarithm of the ratio of any two consecutive peak amplitudes separated by one full time period $T$: $$\delta = \ln\left(\frac{A_n}{A_{n+1}}\right)$$ Since consecutive peak amplitudes follow the exponential envelope $A(t) = A_0 e^{-\gamma t}$, their ratio is: $$\frac{A_n}{A_{n+1}} = \frac{A_0 e^{-\gamma t}}{A_0 e^{-\gamma (t + T)}} = e^{\gamma T}$$ Taking the natural logarithm yields: $$\delta = \ln(e^{\gamma T}) = \gamma T = \frac{\gamma \cdot 2\pi}{\omega}$$ For light damping where $\omega \approx \omega_0$, substituting $\gamma = \frac{\omega_0}{2Q}$ gives the direct relation: $$\delta = \left(\frac{\omega_0}{2Q}\right)\left(\frac{2\pi}{\omega_0}\right) = \frac{\pi}{Q}$$ This equation shows that the logarithmic decrement is inversely proportional to the Quality factor.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Damping directly governs sharpness: low damping yields a narrow, tall peak, while heavy damping flattens the curve.

The sharpness of resonance is quantitatively measured by the Quality Factor ($Q$-factor). By fundamental definition, the $Q$-factor is $2\pi$ times the ratio of the total energy stored in the oscillator to the energy dissipated per cycle: $$Q = 2\pi \frac{\text{Stored Energy}}{\text{Energy Dissipated Per Cycle}} = \frac{\omega_0}{2\gamma} = \frac{m \omega_0}{b}$$ In the frequency domain, the $Q$-factor is expressed in terms of the resonant frequency $\omega_0$ and the half-power bandwidth $\Delta \omega = \omega_2 - \omega_1$: $$Q = \frac{\omega_0}{\Delta \omega} = \frac{f_0}{f_2 - f_1}$$ where $\omega_1$ and $\omega_2$ are the lower and upper half-power cut-off frequencies at which the power absorbed drops to half its maximum value, or equivalently where displacement amplitude falls to $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} A_{max}$. A higher $Q$-factor indicates superior frequency selectivity and low energy loss per oscillation cycle.

In decaying underdamped oscillations, the rate of amplitude attenuation is quantified by the Logarithmic Decrement ($\delta$). It is defined as the natural logarithm of the ratio of any two consecutive peak amplitudes separated by one full time period $T$: $$\delta = \ln\left(\frac{A_n}{A_{n+1}}\right)$$ Since consecutive peak amplitudes follow the exponential envelope $A(t) = A_0 e^{-\gamma t}$, their ratio is: $$\frac{A_n}{A_{n+1}} = \frac{A_0 e^{-\gamma t}}{A_0 e^{-\gamma (t + T)}} = e^{\gamma T}$$ Taking the natural logarithm yields: $$\delta = \ln(e^{\gamma T}) = \gamma T = \frac{\gamma \cdot 2\pi}{\omega}$$ For light damping where $\omega \approx \omega_0$, substituting $\gamma = \frac{\omega_0}{2Q}$ gives the direct relation: $$\delta = \left(\frac{\omega_0}{2Q}\right)\left(\frac{2\pi}{\omega_0}\right) = \frac{\pi}{Q}$$ This equation shows that the logarithmic decrement is inversely proportional to the Quality factor.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 1.3: Amplitude Resonance Curves for Low, Medium, and High Damping Factors — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
Question 4 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)

Q4. Derive the one-dimensional differential wave equation for the propagation of transverse waves on a stretched flexible string from fundamental principles. Q4. Derive the one-dimensional differential wave equation for the propagation of transverse waves on a stretched flexible string from fundamental principles. Q4. Derive the one-dimensional differential wave equation for the propagation of transverse waves on a stretched flexible string from fundamental principles.

KTU Exam Source: January 2021 Improvement Exam (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 12(b) (7 Marks)

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = v^2 \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2}, \quad \text{where } v = \sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$$

1D Wave Equation: Relates local acceleration to spatial curvature in a continuous elastic medium.
Wave Velocity on Stretched String: $v = \sqrt{T/\mu}$, where $T$ is tension and $\mu$ is linear mass density.
D'Alembert Solution: $y(x,t) = f(x - vt) + g(x + vt)$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = v^2 \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2}, \quad \text{where } v = \sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$$

1D Wave Equation: Relates local acceleration to spatial curvature in a continuous elastic medium.
Wave Velocity on Stretched String: $v = \sqrt{T/\mu}$, where $T$ is tension and $\mu$ is linear mass density.
D'Alembert Solution: $y(x,t) = f(x - vt) + g(x + vt)$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A mechanical wave is a disturbance that travels through an elastic continuous medium, transferring momentum and energy without the permanent bulk transport of matter. When a stretched flexible string is displaced transversely from its equilibrium position, the tension in the string generates an elastic restoring force that pulls displaced elements back towards equilibrium.

Consider a perfectly flexible, uniform string under constant tension $T$ having a linear mass density (mass per unit length) $\mu$. In its undisturbed equilibrium configuration, the string lies horizontally along the $x$-axis. When plucked, transverse displacements $y(x,t)$ are produced in the vertical $y$-direction. We analyze an infinitesimal segment of the string bounded between spatial coordinates $x$ and $x + \Delta x$. We adopt two standard physical assumptions: the transverse displacements and slopes $\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}$ are sufficiently small such that the tension $T$ remains uniform and constant throughout the motion, and string elements move purely in the vertical transverse direction without longitudinal displacement.

At coordinate $x$, the tension force $T$ acts tangentially to the string at an angle $\theta$ to the horizontal. At coordinate $x + \Delta x$, the tension $T$ acts tangentially at an angle $\theta + \Delta \theta$. Resolving these tension forces into horizontal and vertical components: Horizontal forces: $$\Sigma F_x = T \cos(\theta + \Delta \theta) - T \cos\theta$$ For small angular deflections, $\cos\theta \approx 1$, meaning horizontal forces cancel out and there is zero net horizontal acceleration. Vertical forces: $$\Sigma F_y = T \sin(\theta + \Delta \theta) - T \sin\theta$$ For small angles $\theta$, we apply the small-angle approximation $\sin\theta \approx \tan\theta = \frac{\partial y}{\partial x}$. The net vertical transverse force acting on the segment becomes: $$\Sigma F_y = T \left[ \tan(\theta + \Delta \theta) - \tan\theta \right] = T \left[ \left(\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\right)_{x + \Delta x} - \left(\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\right)_x \right]$$ Using Taylor series expansion or the fundamental definition of the spatial derivative: $$\left(\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\right)_{x + \Delta x} - \left(\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\right)_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\right) \Delta x = \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} \Delta x$$ Substituting this into the vertical force expression yields: $$\Sigma F_y = T \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} \Delta x$$

Now we apply Newton's second law of motion to this differential string element. The mass of the segment is $\Delta m = \mu \Delta x$, and its transverse acceleration is $\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2}$. Therefore: $$\Sigma F_y = \Delta m \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = (\mu \Delta x) \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2}$$ Equating the dynamic inertia force to the net elastic transverse tension force: $$\mu \Delta x \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = T \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} \Delta x$$ Dividing both sides by $\Delta x$: $$\mu \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = T \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} \implies \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = \frac{T}{\mu} \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2}$$ Defining the wave propagation velocity $v = \sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$, this differential relationship takes the canonical form of the Classical One-Dimensional Wave Equation: $$\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2} = v^2 \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} \quad \text{or} \quad \frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} = \frac{1}{v^2}\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2}$$

This partial differential equation connects the temporal acceleration of any medium point to the spatial curvature of the wave profile at that instant. According to D'Alembert's theorem, the general solution of this equation is given by the superposition of two arbitrary traveling wave profiles: $$y(x,t) = f(x - vt) + g(x + vt)$$ where $f(x - vt)$ represents a non-dispersive wave travelling in the positive $x$-direction at speed $v$, and $g(x + vt)$ represents a wave travelling in the negative $x$-direction at the same speed.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Q5. Apply rigid boundary conditions to obtain the standing wave modes and quantized natural frequencies of transverse vibrations on a stretched string fixed at both ends. State Mersenne's laws. Q5. Apply rigid boundary conditions to obtain the standing wave modes and quantized natural frequencies of transverse vibrations on a stretched string fixed at both ends. State Mersenne's laws. Q5. Apply rigid boundary conditions to obtain the standing wave modes and quantized natural frequencies of transverse vibrations on a stretched string fixed at both ends. State Mersenne's laws.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f_n = \frac{n v}{2L} = \frac{n}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}, \quad \lambda_n = \frac{2L}{n}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$

Standing Wave Modes: Fixed ends require nodes at $x = 0$ and $x = L$, giving quantized wave vectors $k_n = n\pi / L$.
Harmonics: Supports all harmonics $f_n = n f_1$ ($f_1 = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{T/\mu}$).

⚡ Key Equation

$$f_n = \frac{n v}{2L} = \frac{n}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}, \quad \lambda_n = \frac{2L}{n}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$

Standing Wave Modes: Fixed ends require nodes at $x = 0$ and $x = L$, giving quantized wave vectors $k_n = n\pi / L$.
Harmonics: Supports all harmonics $f_n = n f_1$ ($f_1 = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{T/\mu}$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f_n = \frac{n v}{2L} = \frac{n}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}, \quad \lambda_n = \frac{2L}{n}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$

Standing Wave Modes: Fixed ends require nodes at $x = 0$ and $x = L$, giving quantized wave vectors $k_n = n\pi / L$.
Harmonics: Supports all harmonics $f_n = n f_1$ ($f_1 = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{T/\mu}$).
Mersenne's Laws: $f \propto 1/L$, $f \propto \sqrt{T}$, $f \propto 1/\sqrt{\mu}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

When two identical transverse harmonic waves of the same frequency and amplitude travel along a stretched string in mutually opposite directions, their linear superposition forms a stationary or standing wave. In a standing wave, energy is trapped within the medium, producing fixed spatial points with zero motion (nodes) and alternating points of maximum oscillation amplitude (antinodes).

Consider a uniform string of length $L$ stretched under tension $T$ with linear mass density $\mu$, rigidly clamped at both ends $x = 0$ and $x = L$. The governing one-dimensional wave equation is: $$\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} = \frac{1}{v^2}\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2}, \quad \text{where } v = \sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$$ Using the method of separation of variables, we express the transverse displacement as a product of a purely spatial function $X(x)$ and a purely temporal function $T(t)$: $$y(x,t) = X(x) \cdot T(t)$$ Differentiating and substituting into the wave equation: $$X''(x) T(t) = \frac{1}{v^2} X(x) \ddot{T}(t) \implies \frac{X''(x)}{X(x)} = \frac{1}{v^2}\frac{\ddot{T}(t)}{T(t)} = -k^2$$ where $-k^2$ is a negative separation constant necessary for oscillatory behavior. This yields two ordinary differential equations: $$X''(x) + k^2 X(x) = 0$$ $$\ddot{T}(t) + \omega^2 T(t) = 0 \quad (\text{where } \omega = v k)$$ The spatial solution is: $$X(x) = C_1 \cos(kx) + C_2 \sin(kx)$$ Now we apply the rigid boundary conditions at the clamped ends of the string: 1. At the left clamped end ($x = 0$): The string cannot move, so $y(0,t) = 0 \implies X(0) = 0$. $$X(0) = C_1 \cos(0) + C_2 \sin(0) = C_1 = 0$$ Thus $C_1 = 0$, leaving $X(x) = C_2 \sin(kx)$. 2. At the right clamped end ($x = L$): The displacement must also be zero, so $y(L,t) = 0 \implies X(L) = 0$. $$X(L) = C_2 \sin(k L) = 0$$ For non-trivial oscillations ($C_2 \neq 0$), the sine argument must be an integer multiple of $\pi$: $$k L = n \pi \implies k_n = \frac{n \pi}{L}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ Since wave vector $k = \frac{2\pi}{\lambda}$, the allowed stationary wavelengths are quantized: $$\lambda_n = \frac{2L}{n}$$

The corresponding quantized natural frequencies of vibration are obtained from $f_n = \frac{v}{\lambda_n}$: $$f_n = \frac{n v}{2L} = \frac{n}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ Analysis of harmonic vibrational modes: 1. Fundamental Mode ($n = 1$): This is the fundamental frequency or first harmonic: $$f_1 = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$$ The string vibrates in a single loop with nodes at the ends ($x = 0, x = L$) and a single antinode at the center ($x = L/2$). 2. Second Harmonic ($n = 2$): Known as the first overtone: $$f_2 = \frac{2}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}} = 2 f_1$$ The string vibrates in two loops with three nodes ($x = 0, L/2, L$) and two antinodes ($x = L/4, 3L/4$). 3. Third Harmonic ($n = 3$): Known as the second overtone: $$f_3 = \frac{3}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}} = 3 f_1$$ In general, $f_n = n f_1$. A stretched string fixed at both ends supports all integer harmonics (both odd and even), creating a rich musical acoustic spectrum.

From the fundamental frequency equation $f = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$, Mersenne formulated the three fundamental empirical laws of transverse vibrations: 1. Law of Length: The fundamental frequency is inversely proportional to the vibrating length: $f \propto \frac{1}{L}$ (for constant $T, \mu$). 2. Law of Tension: The fundamental frequency is directly proportional to the square root of tension: $f \propto \sqrt{T}$ (for constant $L, \mu$). 3. Law of Mass: The fundamental frequency is inversely proportional to the square root of linear mass density: $f \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}$ (for constant $L, T$). If the wire has radius $r$ and density $\rho$, $\mu = \pi r^2 \rho$, leading to $f \propto \frac{1}{r \sqrt{\rho}}$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In a standing wave, energy is trapped within the medium, producing fixed spatial points with zero motion (nodes) and alternating points of maximum oscillation amplitude (antinodes).

Consider a uniform string of length $L$ stretched under tension $T$ with linear mass density $\mu$, rigidly clamped at both ends $x = 0$ and $x = L$. The governing one-dimensional wave equation is: $$\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} = \frac{1}{v^2}\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2}, \quad \text{where } v = \sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$$ Using the method of separation of variables, we express the transverse displacement as a product of a purely spatial function $X(x)$ and a purely temporal function $T(t)$: $$y(x,t) = X(x) \cdot T(t)$$ Differentiating and substituting into the wave equation: $$X''(x) T(t) = \frac{1}{v^2} X(x) \ddot{T}(t) \implies \frac{X''(x)}{X(x)} = \frac{1}{v^2}\frac{\ddot{T}(t)}{T(t)} = -k^2$$ where $-k^2$ is a negative separation constant necessary for oscillatory behavior. This yields two ordinary differential equations: $$X''(x) + k^2 X(x) = 0$$ $$\ddot{T}(t) + \omega^2 T(t) = 0 \quad (\text{where } \omega = v k)$$ The spatial solution is: $$X(x) = C_1 \cos(kx) + C_2 \sin(kx)$$ Now we apply the rigid boundary conditions at the clamped ends of the string: 1. At the left clamped end ($x = 0$): The string cannot move, so $y(0,t) = 0 \implies X(0) = 0$. $$X(0) = C_1 \cos(0) + C_2 \sin(0) = C_1 = 0$$ Thus $C_1 = 0$, leaving $X(x) = C_2 \sin(kx)$. 2. At the right clamped end ($x = L$): The displacement must also be zero, so $y(L,t) = 0 \implies X(L) = 0$. $$X(L) = C_2 \sin(k L) = 0$$ For non-trivial oscillations ($C_2 \neq 0$), the sine argument must be an integer multiple of $\pi$: $$k L = n \pi \implies k_n = \frac{n \pi}{L}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ Since wave vector $k = \frac{2\pi}{\lambda}$, the allowed stationary wavelengths are quantized: $$\lambda_n = \frac{2L}{n}$$

The corresponding quantized natural frequencies of vibration are obtained from $f_n = \frac{v}{\lambda_n}$: $$f_n = \frac{n v}{2L} = \frac{n}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ Analysis of harmonic vibrational modes: 1. Fundamental Mode ($n = 1$): This is the fundamental frequency or first harmonic: $$f_1 = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$$ The string vibrates in a single loop with nodes at the ends ($x = 0, x = L$) and a single antinode at the center ($x = L/2$). 2. Second Harmonic ($n = 2$): Known as the first overtone: $$f_2 = \frac{2}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}} = 2 f_1$$ The string vibrates in two loops with three nodes ($x = 0, L/2, L$) and two antinodes ($x = L/4, 3L/4$). 3. Third Harmonic ($n = 3$): Known as the second overtone: $$f_3 = \frac{3}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}} = 3 f_1$$ In general, $f_n = n f_1$. A stretched string fixed at both ends supports all integer harmonics (both odd and even), creating a rich musical acoustic spectrum.

From the fundamental frequency equation $f = \frac{1}{2L}\sqrt{\frac{T}{\mu}}$, Mersenne formulated the three fundamental empirical laws of transverse vibrations: 1. Law of Length: The fundamental frequency is inversely proportional to the vibrating length: $f \propto \frac{1}{L}$ (for constant $T, \mu$). 2. Law of Tension: The fundamental frequency is directly proportional to the square root of tension: $f \propto \sqrt{T}$ (for constant $L, \mu$). 3. Law of Mass: The fundamental frequency is inversely proportional to the square root of linear mass density: $f \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}$ (for constant $L, T$). If the wire has radius $r$ and density $\rho$, $\mu = \pi r^2 \rho$, leading to $f \propto \frac{1}{r \sqrt{\rho}}$.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 1.5: Standing Wave Modes on a Stretched String (Fundamental, 2nd, and 3rd Harmonics) — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Deduce the conditions for constructive and destructive interference, and explain why an extremely thin film appears dark.

$$\Delta = 2\mu t \cos r - \frac{\lambda}{2} \implies \text{Bright: } 2\mu t \cos r = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}, \quad \text{Dark: } 2\mu t \cos r = n\lambda$$

Stokes' Phase Shift: Reflection at an optically denser medium introduces an equivalent path difference of $\lambda/2$.

$$\Delta = 2\mu t \cos r - \frac{\lambda}{2} \implies \text{Bright: } 2\mu t \cos r = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}, \quad \text{Dark: } 2\mu t \cos r = n\lambda$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Interference in thin films occurs through the division of amplitude of an incident light wave. When a monochromatic light beam strikes the upper surface of a thin transparent film of uniform thickness $t$ and refractive index $\mu$, partial reflection and partial refraction take place. The refracted beam traverses the film, reflects from the bottom surface, and emerges back into the upper medium parallel to the beam reflected from the top surface. Because both rays originate from the same primary wavefront, they are coherent and interfere constructively or destructively depending on their optical path difference.

Consider a parallel light ray $AB$ incident on the top boundary of a dielectric film of thickness $t$ at an angle of incidence $i$. At point $B$, a portion of the ray is reflected along $BR_1$, while another portion is refracted into the film at angle of refraction $r$ along $BC$. At the lower interface $C$, ray $BC$ undergoes internal reflection along $CD$. At point $D$ on the upper surface, the ray is refracted out into the initial medium along $DR_2$, parallel to $BR_1$. To calculate the optical path difference between reflected rays $BR_1$ and $DR_2$, we drop a perpendicular $DN$ from $D$ onto ray $BR_1$. The wavefront is plane after normal $DN$, meaning the optical paths from $D$ and $N$ onwards are identical.

The geometric optical path difference between the two rays up to normal $DN$ is given by the optical length traversed inside the film minus the path traversed in the surrounding medium (air, $\mu_{air} = 1$): $$\Delta_{geom} = \mu (BC + CD) - BN$$ In the right-angled triangle $\Delta BND$, the path in air is: $$BN = BD \sin i$$ From the geometry of the film, let $BM$ be the normal at $B$ and $DM$ be the normal at $D$, meeting at the lower boundary. In the right-angled triangles formed inside the film: $$BC = CD = \frac{t}{\cos r}$$ The horizontal distance between points of entry and exit is: $$BD = 2 t \tan r$$ Substituting $BD$ into the expression for $BN$: $$BN = (2 t \tan r) \sin i$$ By Snell's law of refraction, $\sin i = \mu \sin r$. Therefore: $$BN = 2 t \left(\frac{\sin r}{\cos r}\right) (\mu \sin r) = \frac{2\mu t \sin^2 r}{\cos r}$$ Substituting $BC$, $CD$, and $BN$ into the optical path difference equation: $$\Delta_{geom} = \mu \left( \frac{t}{\cos r} + \frac{t}{\cos r} \right) - \frac{2\mu t \sin^2 r}{\cos r} = \frac{2\mu t}{\cos r} - \frac{2\mu t \sin^2 r}{\cos r} = \frac{2\mu t}{\cos r} (1 - \sin^2 r)$$ Since $1 - \sin^2 r = \cos^2 r$: $$\Delta_{geom} = \frac{2\mu t}{\cos r} \cos^2 r = 2\mu t \cos r$$

Now we must incorporate Stokes' treatment of phase change upon reflection. When light reflects from the boundary of an optically denser medium (at point $B$ in air reflecting from film), it undergoes an abrupt phase change of $\pi$ radians, which is equivalent to introducing an extra optical path difference of $\frac{\lambda}{2}$. Ray $BC$ reflects at point $C$ from a rarer medium boundary (film to air), which introduces no phase shift. Consequently, the total effective optical path difference between rays $BR_1$ and $DR_2$ is: $$\Delta = 2\mu t \cos r - \frac{\lambda}{2} \quad \left(\text{or } 2\mu t \cos r + \frac{\lambda}{2}\right)$$

Conditions for constructive and destructive interference: 1. Condition for Bright Fringes (Constructive Interference / Maxima): Constructive interference occurs when the total optical path difference is an integer multiple of wavelength ($\Delta = n\lambda$): $$2\mu t \cos r - \frac{\lambda}{2} = n\lambda \implies 2\mu t \cos r = \left(n + \frac{1}{2}\right)\lambda = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}, \quad n = 0, 1, 2, \dots$$ 2. Condition for Dark Fringes (Destructive Interference / Minima): Destructive interference occurs when the total optical path difference is an odd half-integral multiple of wavelength ($\Delta = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}$): $$2\mu t \cos r - \frac{\lambda}{2} = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2} \implies 2\mu t \cos r = (n + 1)\lambda = m\lambda, \quad m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$

Physical Consequences: 1. Why an extremely thin film appears dark in reflected light: If the film thickness $t$ is much smaller than the wavelength of light ($t \ll \lambda$, e.g., $t \to 0$ for a newly formed soap bubble), the term $2\mu t \cos r \approx 0$. The effective path difference reduces solely to $\Delta = -\frac{\lambda}{2}$. This corresponds precisely to the condition for destructive interference for all wavelengths in the visible spectrum. Hence, an ultra-thin film appears completely black before it bursts. 2. Colors in thin films illuminated by white light: When a thin film (such as an oil slick on water or a soap bubble) is illuminated by composite white light, light rays of different wavelengths satisfy the constructive interference condition at different film thicknesses and viewing angles ($r$). Certain wavelengths undergo constructive interference and appear brilliant, while complementary wavelengths undergo destructive interference and are extinguished. This creates vibrant interference color patterns across the surface.

Question 7 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: PHY-Q7

Q7. Explain the formation of interference fringes in an air wedge. Derive the expression for fringe width and explain how the diameter of a thin wire is measured experimentally. Q7. Explain the formation of interference fringes in an air wedge. Derive the expression for fringe width and explain how the diameter of a thin wire is measured experimentally. Q7. Explain the formation of interference fringes in an air wedge. Derive the expression for fringe width and explain how the diameter of a thin wire is measured experimentally.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: December 2021 Evaluation Scheme (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 13(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\beta = \frac{\lambda L}{2d} \implies d = \frac{\lambda L}{2\beta}$$

Air Wedge Fringe Width: $\beta = \frac{\lambda}{2\theta} = \frac{\lambda L}{2d}$, producing equidistant, straight, parallel fringes.
Apex Line of Contact: Always a dark fringe ($2t = 0 \cdot \lambda$).
Wire Diameter ($d$): Measured using travelling microscope: $d = \frac{\lambda L}{2\beta}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\beta = \frac{\lambda L}{2d} \implies d = \frac{\lambda L}{2\beta}$$

Air Wedge Fringe Width: $\beta = \frac{\lambda}{2\theta} = \frac{\lambda L}{2d}$, producing equidistant, straight, parallel fringes.
Apex Line of Contact: Always a dark fringe ($2t = 0 \cdot \lambda$).
Wire Diameter ($d$): Measured using travelling microscope: $d = \frac{\lambda L}{2\beta}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

An air wedge consists of a wedge-shaped film of air formed between two optically flat rectangular glass plates. The plates are placed in contact along one edge (the apex line) and separated at the opposite edge by a thin spacer, such as a thin wire or paper strip of diameter $d$, located at a distance $L$ from the apex. This geometry creates an air film whose thickness increases continuously and linearly from zero at the contact apex to $d$ at the spacer edge.

When monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ falls normally ($i = 0 \implies r = 0$, so $\cos r = 1$) on the upper glass plate, partial reflection occurs at the bottom surface of the upper plate and the top surface of the lower plate. These two reflected beams are coherent and interfere to produce straight, equidistant, parallel interference fringes localized in the air film, oriented parallel to the line of contact. For an air film ($\mu = 1$), the optical path difference at any point where the air film thickness is $t$ is: $$\Delta = 2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2}$$ where the additional term $\frac{\lambda}{2}$ arises from the phase reversal ($\pi$ radians) suffered by the ray reflecting from the top surface of the lower glass plate (air-to-glass interface).

Conditions for interference fringes across the wedge: 1. Condition for Dark Fringes: Destructive interference occurs when the path difference equals an odd half-integral multiple of wavelength: $$2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2} = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2} \implies 2 t = n\lambda, \quad n = 0, 1, 2, \dots$$ At the contact apex ($t = 0$), $2(0) = 0 = 0 \cdot \lambda$ (corresponding to $n = 0$), which satisfies the dark fringe condition. Consequently, the edge of contact is always a dark fringe. 2. Condition for Bright Fringes: Constructive interference occurs when: $$2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2} = n\lambda \implies 2 t = \left(n - \frac{1}{2}\right)\lambda = (2n - 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$

Derivation of Fringe Width ($\beta$): Let the wedge angle between the two glass plates be $\theta$ (in radians). Since $\theta$ is very small, we have: $$\theta = \frac{d}{L} \approx \tan\theta$$ Let $x_n$ be the distance of the $n$-th dark fringe from the apex line of contact, where the air film thickness is $t_n$. From wedge geometry: $$t_n = x_n \tan\theta \approx x_n \theta$$ Substituting this into the dark fringe condition $2 t_n = n\lambda$: $$2 (x_n \theta) = n\lambda \implies x_n = \frac{n\lambda}{2\theta}$$ Similarly, for the $(n + 1)$-th consecutive dark fringe situated at distance $x_{n+1}$: $$x_{n+1} = \frac{(n + 1)\lambda}{2\theta}$$ The fringe width $\beta$ is defined as the linear distance between two consecutive dark or bright fringes: $$\beta = x_{n+1} - x_n = \frac{(n + 1)\lambda}{2\theta} - \frac{n\lambda}{2\theta} = \frac{\lambda}{2\theta}$$ Substituting the geometric relation $\theta = \frac{d}{L}$ yields: $$\beta = \frac{\lambda L}{2d}$$ This fundamental expression proves that the fringe width $\beta$ is constant throughout the length of the wedge, meaning the interference fringes are perfectly equidistant and straight.

Determination of the Diameter / Thickness of a Thin Wire ($d$): The air wedge configuration provides an exceptionally sensitive non-contact optical method for measuring the diameter of a thin wire or the thickness of thin micro-foils. The experimental procedure is as follows: 1. The thin wire of unknown diameter $d$ is inserted between two clean optical flat glass plates at an exact distance $L$ from the edge of contact. 2. Monochromatic light from a sodium vapor lamp ($\lambda = 589.3\text{ nm}$) is reflected normally onto the wedge using a glass plate inclined at $45^\circ$. 3. The resulting straight, parallel interference fringes are observed using a travelling microscope equipped with a vernier scale. 4. The cross-wire of the microscope is aligned with a particular dark fringe (say the $n$-th fringe), and the micrometer reading $R_1$ is recorded. The microscope is then traversed across $N$ fringes (e.g., $N = 20$ or $30$) to the $(n + N)$-th fringe, and the reading $R_2$ is recorded. 5. The average fringe width is calculated as: $$\beta = \frac{|R_2 - R_1|}{N}$$ 6. Rearranging the fringe width formula $\beta = \frac{\lambda L}{2d}$, the wire diameter $d$ is computed directly as: $$d = \frac{\lambda L}{2\beta}$$ This method achieves sub-micron measurement accuracy without applying mechanical compression that could deform delicate specimens.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

An air wedge consists of a wedge-shaped film of air formed between two optically flat rectangular glass plates. The plates are placed in contact along one edge (the apex line) and separated at the opposite edge by a thin spacer, such as a thin wire or paper strip of diameter $d$, located at a distance $L$ from the apex. This geometry creates an air film whose thickness increases continuously and linearly from zero at the contact apex to $d$ at the spacer edge.

When monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ falls normally ($i = 0 \implies r = 0$, so $\cos r = 1$) on the upper glass plate, partial reflection occurs at the bottom surface of the upper plate and the top surface of the lower plate. These two reflected beams are coherent and interfere to produce straight, equidistant, parallel interference fringes localized in the air film, oriented parallel to the line of contact. For an air film ($\mu = 1$), the optical path difference at any point where the air film thickness is $t$ is: $$\Delta = 2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2}$$ where the additional term $\frac{\lambda}{2}$ arises from the phase reversal ($\pi$ radians) suffered by the ray reflecting from the top surface of the lower glass plate (air-to-glass interface).

Conditions for interference fringes across the wedge: 1. Condition for Dark Fringes: Destructive interference occurs when the path difference equals an odd half-integral multiple of wavelength: $$2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2} = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2} \implies 2 t = n\lambda, \quad n = 0, 1, 2, \dots$$ At the contact apex ($t = 0$), $2(0) = 0 = 0 \cdot \lambda$ (corresponding to $n = 0$), which satisfies the dark fringe condition. Consequently, the edge of contact is always a dark fringe. 2. Condition for Bright Fringes: Constructive interference occurs when: $$2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2} = n\lambda \implies 2 t = \left(n - \frac{1}{2}\right)\lambda = (2n - 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$

Derivation of Fringe Width ($\beta$): Let the wedge angle between the two glass plates be $\theta$ (in radians). Since $\theta$ is very small, we have: $$\theta = \frac{d}{L} \approx \tan\theta$$ Let $x_n$ be the distance of the $n$-th dark fringe from the apex line of contact, where the air film thickness is $t_n$. From wedge geometry: $$t_n = x_n \tan\theta \approx x_n \theta$$ Substituting this into the dark fringe condition $2 t_n = n\lambda$: $$2 (x_n \theta) = n\lambda \implies x_n = \frac{n\lambda}{2\theta}$$ Similarly, for the $(n + 1)$-th consecutive dark fringe situated at distance $x_{n+1}$: $$x_{n+1} = \frac{(n + 1)\lambda}{2\theta}$$ The fringe width $\beta$ is defined as the linear distance between two consecutive dark or bright fringes: $$\beta = x_{n+1} - x_n = \frac{(n + 1)\lambda}{2\theta} - \frac{n\lambda}{2\theta} = \frac{\lambda}{2\theta}$$ Substituting the geometric relation $\theta = \frac{d}{L}$ yields: $$\beta = \frac{\lambda L}{2d}$$ This fundamental expression proves that the fringe width $\beta$ is constant throughout the length of the wedge, meaning the interference fringes are perfectly equidistant and straight.

Determination of the Diameter / Thickness of a Thin Wire ($d$): The air wedge configuration provides an exceptionally sensitive non-contact optical method for measuring the diameter of a thin wire or the thickness of thin micro-foils. The experimental procedure is as follows: 1. The thin wire of unknown diameter $d$ is inserted between two clean optical flat glass plates at an exact distance $L$ from the edge of contact. 2. Monochromatic light from a sodium vapor lamp ($\lambda = 589.3\text{ nm}$) is reflected normally onto the wedge using a glass plate inclined at $45^\circ$. 3. The resulting straight, parallel interference fringes are observed using a travelling microscope equipped with a vernier scale. 4. The cross-wire of the microscope is aligned with a particular dark fringe (say the $n$-th fringe), and the micrometer reading $R_1$ is recorded. The microscope is then traversed across $N$ fringes (e.g., $N = 20$ or $30$) to the $(n + N)$-th fringe, and the reading $R_2$ is recorded. 5. The average fringe width is calculated as: $$\beta = \frac{|R_2 - R_1|}{N}$$ 6. Rearranging the fringe width formula $\beta = \frac{\lambda L}{2d}$, the wire diameter $d$ is computed directly as: $$d = \frac{\lambda L}{2\beta}$$ This method achieves sub-micron measurement accuracy without applying mechanical compression that could deform delicate specimens.

Question 8 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: PHY-Q8

Q8. Explain the theory of Newton's rings in reflected light. Derive expressions for the diameters of dark and bright rings, and explain how the wavelength of sodium light and the refractive index of a liquid are determined. Q8. Explain the theory of Newton's rings in reflected light. Derive expressions for the diameters of dark and bright rings, and explain how the wavelength of sodium light and the refractive index of a liquid are determined. Q8. Explain the theory of Newton's rings in reflected light. Derive expressions for the diameters of dark and bright rings, and explain how the wavelength of sodium light and the refractive index of a liquid are determined.

KTU Exam Source: December 2019 University Exam (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 14(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$D_n^2 = 4n R \lambda \text{ (Dark)}, \quad \lambda = \frac{D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2}{4 p R}, \quad \mu = \frac{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{air}}{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid}}$$

Air Film Thickness: $t = r^2 / 2R$.
Dark Ring Diameters: $D_n \propto \sqrt{n}$ ($D_n = 2\sqrt{n R \lambda}$); center is dark ($D_0 = 0$).
Bright Ring Diameters: $D_n \propto \sqrt{2n - 1}$.
Liquid Film: Fringes contract when liquid of index $\mu > 1$ is introduced ($D_n \propto 1/\sqrt{\mu}$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Newton's rings are circular interference fringes formed when a plano-convex lens of large radius of curvature $R$ is placed with its curved surface resting on an optically flat glass plate. This arrangement encloses a circularly symmetric air film whose thickness is zero at the central point of contact and increases symmetrically in all radial directions outward as the square of the distance from the contact point.

When monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ falls normally on this setup, light reflects from two interfaces: the curved lower surface of the lens and the top surface of the flat plate. These coherent reflected waves interfere to produce concentric circular bright and dark rings centered on the point of contact. Let $t$ be the thickness of the air film at a radial distance $r$ from the center of contact. From the geometry of a circle, if $R$ is the radius of curvature of the spherical lens surface, applying the intersecting chord theorem gives: $$r^2 = t (2R - t) = 2R t - t^2$$ Because $R$ is typically 1 to 2 meters while $t$ is on the order of microns, $t \ll R$. Hence the $t^2$ term is negligible compared to $2Rt$: $$r^2 \approx 2R t \implies t = \frac{r^2}{2R}$$ For normal incidence in an air film ($\mu = 1, r = 0, \cos r = 1$), the total effective optical path difference between the interfering rays in the reflected system is: $$\Delta = 2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2} = 2\left(\frac{r^2}{2R}\right) + \frac{\lambda}{2} = \frac{r^2}{R} + \frac{\lambda}{2}$$ where $\frac{\lambda}{2}$ accounts for the phase shift of $\pi$ radians upon reflection from the optically denser flat glass plate.

Derivation of Diameters for Dark and Bright Rings: 1. Dark Rings (Destructive Interference): Condition for destructive interference is $\Delta = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}$. $$\frac{r_n^2}{R} + \frac{\lambda}{2} = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2} \implies \frac{r_n^2}{R} = n\lambda \implies r_n^2 = n R \lambda$$ Expressed in terms of the ring diameter $D_n = 2 r_n$ (so $D_n^2 = 4 r_n^2$): $$D_n^2 = 4 n R \lambda \implies D_n = 2\sqrt{n R \lambda} \propto \sqrt{n}, \quad n = 0, 1, 2, \dots$$ The diameters of dark rings are directly proportional to the square roots of natural integers ($0, 1, 2, 3, \dots$). At the central contact point ($n = 0$), $D_0 = 0$, meaning the central spot is always perfectly dark. 2. Bright Rings (Constructive Interference): Condition for constructive interference is $\Delta = n\lambda$. $$\frac{r_n^2}{R} + \frac{\lambda}{2} = n\lambda \implies \frac{r_n^2}{R} = \left(n - \frac{1}{2}\right)\lambda = (2n - 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}$$ $$D_n^2 = 4(2n - 1)\frac{R\lambda}{2} = 2(2n - 1)R\lambda \implies D_n = \sqrt{2(2n - 1)R\lambda} \propto \sqrt{2n - 1}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ The diameters of bright rings are proportional to the square roots of consecutive odd numbers ($1, 3, 5, \dots$). As ring order $n$ increases, the rings become progressively closer together because $\sqrt{n + 1} - \sqrt{n}$ decreases with increasing $n$.

Experimental Determination of Wavelength of Sodium Light ($\lambda$): Measuring ring diameters eliminates errors arising from uncertainty in identifying the exact center. Let $D_n$ be the diameter of the $n$-th dark ring and $D_{n+p}$ be the diameter of the $(n + p)$-th dark ring: $$D_n^2 = 4 n R \lambda$$ $$D_{n+p}^2 = 4 (n + p) R \lambda$$ Subtracting the first equation from the second: $$D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2 = 4 (n + p) R \lambda - 4 n R \lambda = 4 p R \lambda$$ Solving for the wavelength $\lambda$: $$\lambda = \frac{D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2}{4 p R}$$ Using a travelling microscope, the diameters of several dark rings (e.g., $n = 5$ to $20$) are recorded. A graph plotted between ring number $n$ on the $x$-axis and $D_n^2$ on the $y$-axis yields a straight line with slope $S = \frac{D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2}{p} = 4 R \lambda$. The radius of curvature $R$ of the lens is measured independently using a spherometer. The wavelength is then computed accurately as $\lambda = \frac{S}{4R}$.

Determination of Refractive Index of a Liquid ($\mu$): To determine the refractive index $\mu$ of a transparent liquid, a few drops of the liquid are introduced between the plano-convex lens and the flat plate. The liquid displaces the air film, forming a liquid film of refractive index $\mu$. For a liquid film, the path difference for dark rings becomes $2\mu t = n\lambda$, which leads to: $$D_n^2 = \frac{4 n R \lambda}{\mu} \implies (D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid} = \frac{4 p R \lambda}{\mu}$$ Taking the ratio of the diameter-squared differences measured in air and in the liquid: $$\frac{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{air}}{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid}} = \frac{4 p R \lambda}{\frac{4 p R \lambda}{\mu}} = \mu$$ Hence: $$\mu = \frac{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{air}}{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid}}$$ Because $\mu > 1$, the rings contract and shift inwards when liquid is introduced.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Newton's rings are circular interference fringes formed when a plano-convex lens of large radius of curvature $R$ is placed with its curved surface resting on an optically flat glass plate. This arrangement encloses a circularly symmetric air film whose thickness is zero at the central point of contact and increases symmetrically in all radial directions outward as the square of the distance from the contact point.

When monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ falls normally on this setup, light reflects from two interfaces: the curved lower surface of the lens and the top surface of the flat plate. These coherent reflected waves interfere to produce concentric circular bright and dark rings centered on the point of contact. Let $t$ be the thickness of the air film at a radial distance $r$ from the center of contact. From the geometry of a circle, if $R$ is the radius of curvature of the spherical lens surface, applying the intersecting chord theorem gives: $$r^2 = t (2R - t) = 2R t - t^2$$ Because $R$ is typically 1 to 2 meters while $t$ is on the order of microns, $t \ll R$. Hence the $t^2$ term is negligible compared to $2Rt$: $$r^2 \approx 2R t \implies t = \frac{r^2}{2R}$$ For normal incidence in an air film ($\mu = 1, r = 0, \cos r = 1$), the total effective optical path difference between the interfering rays in the reflected system is: $$\Delta = 2 t + \frac{\lambda}{2} = 2\left(\frac{r^2}{2R}\right) + \frac{\lambda}{2} = \frac{r^2}{R} + \frac{\lambda}{2}$$ where $\frac{\lambda}{2}$ accounts for the phase shift of $\pi$ radians upon reflection from the optically denser flat glass plate.

Derivation of Diameters for Dark and Bright Rings: 1. Dark Rings (Destructive Interference): Condition for destructive interference is $\Delta = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}$. $$\frac{r_n^2}{R} + \frac{\lambda}{2} = (2n + 1)\frac{\lambda}{2} \implies \frac{r_n^2}{R} = n\lambda \implies r_n^2 = n R \lambda$$ Expressed in terms of the ring diameter $D_n = 2 r_n$ (so $D_n^2 = 4 r_n^2$): $$D_n^2 = 4 n R \lambda \implies D_n = 2\sqrt{n R \lambda} \propto \sqrt{n}, \quad n = 0, 1, 2, \dots$$ The diameters of dark rings are directly proportional to the square roots of natural integers ($0, 1, 2, 3, \dots$). At the central contact point ($n = 0$), $D_0 = 0$, meaning the central spot is always perfectly dark. 2. Bright Rings (Constructive Interference): Condition for constructive interference is $\Delta = n\lambda$. $$\frac{r_n^2}{R} + \frac{\lambda}{2} = n\lambda \implies \frac{r_n^2}{R} = \left(n - \frac{1}{2}\right)\lambda = (2n - 1)\frac{\lambda}{2}$$ $$D_n^2 = 4(2n - 1)\frac{R\lambda}{2} = 2(2n - 1)R\lambda \implies D_n = \sqrt{2(2n - 1)R\lambda} \propto \sqrt{2n - 1}, \quad n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ The diameters of bright rings are proportional to the square roots of consecutive odd numbers ($1, 3, 5, \dots$). As ring order $n$ increases, the rings become progressively closer together because $\sqrt{n + 1} - \sqrt{n}$ decreases with increasing $n$.

Experimental Determination of Wavelength of Sodium Light ($\lambda$): Measuring ring diameters eliminates errors arising from uncertainty in identifying the exact center. Let $D_n$ be the diameter of the $n$-th dark ring and $D_{n+p}$ be the diameter of the $(n + p)$-th dark ring: $$D_n^2 = 4 n R \lambda$$ $$D_{n+p}^2 = 4 (n + p) R \lambda$$ Subtracting the first equation from the second: $$D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2 = 4 (n + p) R \lambda - 4 n R \lambda = 4 p R \lambda$$ Solving for the wavelength $\lambda$: $$\lambda = \frac{D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2}{4 p R}$$ Using a travelling microscope, the diameters of several dark rings (e.g., $n = 5$ to $20$) are recorded. A graph plotted between ring number $n$ on the $x$-axis and $D_n^2$ on the $y$-axis yields a straight line with slope $S = \frac{D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2}{p} = 4 R \lambda$. The radius of curvature $R$ of the lens is measured independently using a spherometer. The wavelength is then computed accurately as $\lambda = \frac{S}{4R}$.

Determination of Refractive Index of a Liquid ($\mu$): To determine the refractive index $\mu$ of a transparent liquid, a few drops of the liquid are introduced between the plano-convex lens and the flat plate. The liquid displaces the air film, forming a liquid film of refractive index $\mu$. For a liquid film, the path difference for dark rings becomes $2\mu t = n\lambda$, which leads to: $$D_n^2 = \frac{4 n R \lambda}{\mu} \implies (D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid} = \frac{4 p R \lambda}{\mu}$$ Taking the ratio of the diameter-squared differences measured in air and in the liquid: $$\frac{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{air}}{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid}} = \frac{4 p R \lambda}{\frac{4 p R \lambda}{\mu}} = \mu$$ Hence: $$\mu = \frac{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{air}}{(D_{n+p}^2 - D_n^2)_{liquid}}$$ Because $\mu > 1$, the rings contract and shift inwards when liquid is introduced.

KTU Exam Source: January 2021 Improvement Exam (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 14(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I(\theta) = I_0 \left(\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}\right)^2, \quad \alpha = \frac{\pi a \sin\theta}{\lambda}, \quad \text{Minima: } a \sin\theta = m\lambda$$

Fraunhofer Single Slit: Sinc-squared intensity pattern.
Central Maximum: Width $2\theta_1 = 2\lambda/a$, contains $>90\%$ of total optical energy.
Diffraction Minima: $a \sin\theta = \pm m\lambda$ ($m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$).
Secondary Maxima: Occur at $\tan\alpha = \alpha$ with rapidly falling intensities ($I_1 \approx 4.7\% I_0, I_2 \approx 1.65\% I_0$).

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I(\theta) = I_0 \left(\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}\right)^2, \quad \alpha = \frac{\pi a \sin\theta}{\lambda}, \quad \text{Minima: } a \sin\theta = m\lambda$$

Fraunhofer Single Slit: Sinc-squared intensity pattern.

Diffraction Minima: $a \sin\theta = \pm m\lambda$ ($m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$).
Secondary Maxima: Occur at $\tan\alpha = \alpha$ with rapidly falling intensities ($I_1 \approx 4.7\% I_0, I_2 \approx 1.65\% I_0$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Fraunhofer diffraction occurs when both the light source and the observation screen are placed at effectively infinite distances from the diffracting aperture, meaning incident and diffracted wavefronts are completely planar. This condition is realized experimentally using two convex lenses: a collimating lens that converts light from a point source into a plane wavefront, and a focusing lens that converges the diffracted plane wavelets onto an observation screen.

Consider a long narrow slit $AB$ of width $a$ illuminated normally by a monochromatic plane wave of wavelength $\lambda$. According to Huygens-Fresnel principle, every point across the exposed slit width acts as a continuous source of secondary spherical wavelets radiating in all forward directions. To evaluate the resultant electric field at a point $P$ on the screen situated at a diffraction angle $\theta$ relative to the incident normal, we divide the slit width $a$ into $N$ equal infinitesimal strips of width $dx = a / N$, each contributing an electric field amplitude $dE_0 = \frac{E_0}{a} dx$.

Let the origin $x = 0$ be placed at the center of the slit, so coordinates range from $x = -a/2$ to $x = +a/2$. The path difference between a wavelet originating from position $x$ and the wavelet from the central origin ($x = 0$) is $x \sin\theta$. The corresponding phase difference is: $$\phi(x) = \frac{2\pi}{\lambda} x \sin\theta$$ The differential complex electric field contributed by the strip at position $x$ is: $$dE = \left(\frac{E_0}{a} dx\right) e^{i(\omega t - \phi(x))} = \frac{E_0}{a} e^{i\omega t} e^{-i \frac{2\pi}{\lambda} x \sin\theta} dx$$ Integrating across the entire width of the slit from $-a/2$ to $+a/2$: $$E = \frac{E_0}{a} e^{i\omega t} \int_{-a/2}^{a/2} e^{-i \left(\frac{2\pi \sin\theta}{\lambda}\right) x} dx$$ To simplify the algebra, define the dimensionless diffraction parameter $\alpha$: $$\alpha = \frac{\pi a \sin\theta}{\lambda}$$ The phase exponent becomes $-\frac{2\alpha}{a} x$. Performing the integration: $$E = \frac{E_0}{a} e^{i\omega t} \left[ \frac{e^{-i \frac{2\alpha}{a} x}}{-i \frac{2\alpha}{a}} \right]_{-a/2}^{a/2} = \frac{E_0}{a} e^{i\omega t} \frac{e^{-i\alpha} - e^{i\alpha}}{-i \frac{2\alpha}{a}} = E_0 e^{i\omega t} \frac{e^{i\alpha} - e^{-i\alpha}}{2i \alpha}$$ Using the complex exponential definition of the sine function $\sin\alpha = \frac{e^{i\alpha} - e^{-i\alpha}}{2i}$: $$E = E_0 e^{i\omega t} \frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}$$ The optical intensity $I(\theta)$ is proportional to the square of the electric field amplitude: $$I(\theta) = I_0 \left( \frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha} \right)^2 = I_0 \operatorname{sinc}^2(\alpha)$$ where $I_0 \propto E_0^2$ is the peak intensity at the central optical axis ($\theta = 0$).

Conditions for Maxima and Minima in the Intensity Pattern: 1. Central Maximum: When $\theta = 0$, $\alpha = \frac{\pi a \sin(0)}{\lambda} = 0$. Using L'Hôpital's rule: $$\lim_{\alpha \to 0} \frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha} = 1 \implies I(0) = I_0$$ This produces a wide, extremely bright central band known as the principal or central maximum. 2. Diffraction Minima (Zero Intensity): Intensity drops to zero whenever $\sin\alpha = 0$, with $\alpha \neq 0$: $$\alpha = \pm m \pi \implies \frac{\pi a \sin\theta}{\lambda} = \pm m \pi \implies a \sin\theta = \pm m \lambda, \quad m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$$ Minima occur when the path difference between wavelets from the top and bottom edges of the slit is an integer multiple of wavelength ($a \sin\theta = m\lambda$). 3. Secondary Maxima: To find positions of secondary brightness, we differentiate $I(\alpha)$ with respect to $\alpha$ and equate to zero: $$\frac{dI}{d\alpha} = I_0 \frac{d}{d\alpha}\left[\frac{\sin^2\alpha}{\alpha^2}\right] = 2 I_0 \frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha} \left[ \frac{\alpha \cos\alpha - \sin\alpha}{\alpha^2} \right] = 0$$ Excluding $\sin\alpha = 0$ (which gives minima), the secondary maxima condition is: $$\alpha \cos\alpha - \sin\alpha = 0 \implies \tan\alpha = \alpha$$ The transcendental equation $\tan\alpha = \alpha$ is solved graphically by finding the intersection points of $y = \tan\alpha$ and $y = \alpha$. The solutions are $\alpha_1 \approx \pm 1.430\pi \approx \pm 4.493\text{ rad}$, $\alpha_2 \approx \pm 2.459\pi \approx \pm 7.725\text{ rad}$, etc. Evaluating intensities of these secondary maxima relative to $I_0$: $$I_1 = I_0 \left(\frac{\sin 1.43\pi}{1.43\pi}\right)^2 \approx \frac{I_0}{(1.43\pi)^2} \approx 0.0472 I_0 \approx 4.7\% \text{ of } I_0$$ $$I_2 = I_0 \left(\frac{\sin 2.46\pi}{2.46\pi}\right)^2 \approx \frac{I_0}{(2.46\pi)^2} \approx 0.0165 I_0 \approx 1.65\% \text{ of } I_0$$ This demonstrates that over 90% of the total diffracted optical energy is concentrated inside the central maximum, while secondary peaks fall off precipitously.

Angular Width of the Central Maximum: The first diffraction minimum occurs at $a \sin\theta_1 = \lambda$. Since $\theta_1$ is small, $\sin\theta_1 \approx \theta_1 = \frac{\lambda}{a}$. The total angular width $2\theta_1$ extending from the first minimum on one side to the first minimum on the other is: $$2\theta_1 = \frac{2\lambda}{a}$$ If a lens of focal length $f$ focuses the diffraction pattern onto a screen, the linear width of the central maximum is: $$W = 2 f \theta_1 = \frac{2 f \lambda}{a}$$ Narrowing the slit width $a$ causes the central maximum to expand dramatically, demonstrating the wave nature of light.

Question 10 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: PHY-Q10

Q10. State and prove the plane transmission diffraction grating equation. Derive expressions for the maximum observable order of spectra, dispersive power, and resolving power of the grating based on Rayleigh's criterion. Q10. State and prove the plane transmission diffraction grating equation. 🏛️
KTU Exam Source: December 2021 Evaluation Scheme (PHT100) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 14(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$(a + b)\sin\theta = n\lambda, \quad \frac{d\theta}{d\lambda} = \frac{n}{(a+b)\cos\theta}, \quad R = \frac{\lambda}{d\lambda} = n N$$

Grating Equation: $(a + b)\sin\theta = n\lambda$, where $d = a + b$ is the grating element.
Max Order: $n_{max} = \lfloor (a+b)/\lambda \rfloor$.
Dispersive Power: $\frac{d\theta}{d\lambda} = \frac{n}{(a+b)\cos\theta}$ (linear normal spectrum).
Resolving Power: $R = \lambda / d\lambda = n N$ (Rayleigh's criterion, product of order $n$ and total rulings $N$).

⚡ Key Equation

$$(a + b)\sin\theta = n\lambda, \quad \frac{d\theta}{d\lambda} = \frac{n}{(a+b)\cos\theta}, \quad R = \frac{\lambda}{d\lambda} = n N$$

Grating Equation: $(a + b)\sin\theta = n\lambda$, where $d = a + b$ is the grating element.

Dispersive Power: $\frac{d\theta}{d\lambda} = \frac{n}{(a+b)\cos\theta}$ (linear normal spectrum).
